People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came in because things have been getting... strange at work, I guess. I’m a cashier, and lately when I’m at the register I keep getting stuck doing things that don’t really fit what’s happening. Like I’ll be scanning items and then I’ll start tapping the scanner in a certain pattern, not because I need to, just... I get caught in it. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just sort of freeze over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting and staring at me.

It’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to just keep moving, ring them up, hand them the receipt, all of that. I’ve done this job long enough. But in the moment I get stuck, like my body is doing the wrong thing or not doing the next thing. Last week it happened enough that my manager wrote me up, and that’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone instead of just trying to pretend it’s not happening.

Yeah... yeah, it has. It’s not just that one awkward moment with the customer waiting, although that’s bad enough. It sort of spills over. At work people have started treating me like I’m unreliable, or like they have to watch me, and I can feel that. My manager put me on a formal warning now, so it’s not just in my head that it’s a problem there. It’s on paper.

And outside work, I’ve been pulling back more than I want to admit. I used to keep up with friends better, make kayaking plans when the weather was decent, call my family regularly. Lately I’ll see a message or think, “I should call,” and then I just don’t. Or I put it off until it feels too late to respond normally. So it’s affecting people around me, yeah. I’m not keeping up with them the way I used to.

I mean, the clearest place it happens is at work, at the register. I used to keep up with friends better, make kayaking plans when the weather was decent, call my family regularly. Lately I’ll see a message or think, “I should call,” and then I just don’t. Or I put it off until it feels too late to respond normally. So it’s affecting people around me, yeah. I’m not keeping up with them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these patterns of getting stuck or doing things in a certain repetitive way when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, the clearest place it happens is at work, at the register. That’s where it’s been obvious enough that other people notice, because there’s a customer right there and I’m supposed to be scanning or making change, and instead I’ll get caught tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer like I forgot how to move to the next step.\n\nOutside of work... I don’t know if I notice it the same way, maybe because there isn’t always someone standing there waiting on me. But the work stuff is what made it impossible to ignore. Last week it happened enough that I got written up, and that kind of scared me, because I can’t really explain it as just being tired or distracted anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself doing repetitive things, like tapping or repeating actions, when you're alone or not at work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"I came in because at the register I keep getting stuck doing things that don’t fit the moment, like tapping the scanner in a pattern or freezing with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is waiting, and last week it got me written up.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
When I’m alone, it’s harder for me to say for sure. I don’t notice it in the same obvious way, because there isn’t a customer standing there waiting on me or a line forming, so there’s not that immediate pressure that makes it stand out.

The main place it’s really happening is at the register. I’ll be doing something normal, like scanning items, and then I get caught tapping the scanner in a pattern, like my hand just keeps doing that instead of moving on. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just freeze over the cash drawer, and I know the person is waiting, but I don’t move right away. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing in that moment.

That’s really why I came in. It got bad enough last week that my manager wrote me up for it, and that made it feel like, okay, this isn’t just me being a little distracted or having a weird day. It’s becoming a real problem.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure I’d call it anxiety, at least not at the very start of it. It’s more like I suddenly realize I’m not moving forward, and then I become very aware that someone is waiting on me. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.

But it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. I don’t know if I’m anxious and then I get stuck, or if I get stuck and then the anxiety comes because I can see the situation going wrong. Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? Just move your hand, just finish the transaction,” and that makes me feel more pressured.

So... yes, I feel anxious during it, but I don’t know if anxiety is causing it. It feels more like panic after I notice I’m stuck, if that makes sense.

My manager put me on a formal warning now, so it’s not just in my head that it’s a problem there. It’s on paper.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been pulling back more than I want to admit. I used to keep up with friends better, make kayaking plans when the weather was decent, call my family regularly. Lately I’ll see a message or think, “I should call,” and then I just don’t. Or I put it off until it feels too late to respond normally. So it’s affecting people around me, yeah. I’m not keeping up with them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these patterns of getting stuck or doing things in a certain repetitive way when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, the clearest place it happens is at work, at the register. That’s where it’s been obvious enough that other people notice, because there’s a customer right there and I’m supposed to be scanning or making change, and instead I’ll get caught tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer like I forgot how to move to the next step.\n\nOutside of work... I don’t know if I notice it the same way, maybe because there isn’t always someone standing there waiting on me. But the work stuff is what made it impossible to ignore. Last week it happened enough that I got written up, and that kind of scared me, because I can’t really explain it as just being tired or distracted anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing repetitive things, like tapping or repeating actions, when you're alone or not at work?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s harder for me to say for sure. I don’t notice it in the same obvious way, because there isn’t a customer standing there waiting on me or a line forming, so there’s not that immediate pressure that makes it stand out.\n\nThe main place it’s really happening is at the register. I’ll be doing something normal, like scanning items, and then I get caught tapping the scanner in a pattern, like my hand just keeps doing that instead of moving on. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just freeze over the cash drawer, and I know the person is waiting, but I don’t move right away. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing in that moment.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It got bad enough last week that my manager wrote me up for it, and that made it feel like, okay, this isn’t just me being a little distracted or having a weird day. It’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes of getting stuck happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’d call it anxiety, at least not at the very start of it. It’s more like I suddenly realize I’m not moving forward, and then I become very aware that someone is waiting on me. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. I don’t know if I’m anxious and then I get stuck, or if I get stuck and then the anxiety comes because I can see the situation going wrong. Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? Just move your hand, just finish the transaction,” and that makes me feel more pressured.\n\nSo... yes, I feel anxious during it, but I don’t know if anxiety is causing it. It feels more like panic after I notice I’m stuck, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s like I’m doing it because I believe something bad will happen if I don’t, no. It’s not like, “I have to tap the scanner three times or something terrible will happen.” At least, I don’t experience it that way.

It’s more that I’ll be at the register, in the middle of a normal transaction, and then I get stuck doing something that doesn’t really fit what’s going on. Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.

That’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to make things feel “right” or prevent something. It’s more like my actions get stuck in the wrong place.

I’m a cashier, and lately when I’m at the register I keep getting stuck doing things that don’t really fit what’s happening. Like I’ll be scanning items and then I’ll start tapping the scanner in a certain pattern, not because I need to, just... I get caught in it. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just sort of freeze over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting and staring at me.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to just keep moving, ring them up, hand them the receipt, all of that. I’ve done this job long enough. But in the moment I get stuck, like my body is doing the wrong thing or not doing the next thing. Last week it happened enough that my manager wrote me up, and that’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone instead of just trying to pretend it’s not happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these episodes of getting stuck are affecting your interactions with customers or coworkers outside of just the immediate task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, it has. It’s not just that one awkward moment with the customer waiting, although that’s bad enough. It sort of spills over. At work people have started treating me like I’m unreliable, or like they have to watch me, and I can feel that. My manager put me on a formal warning now, so it’s not just in my head that it’s a problem there. It’s on paper.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been pulling back more than I want to admit. I used to keep up with friends better, make kayaking plans when the weather was decent, call my family regularly. Lately I’ll see a message or think, “I should call,” and then I just don’t. Or I put it off until it feels too late to respond normally. So it’s affecting people around me, yeah. I’m not keeping up with them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these patterns of getting stuck or doing things in a certain repetitive way when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, the clearest place it happens is at work, at the register. That’s where it’s been obvious enough that other people notice, because there’s a customer right there and I’m supposed to be scanning or making change, and instead I’ll get caught tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer like I forgot how to move to the next step.\n\nOutside of work... I don’t know if I notice it the same way, maybe because there isn’t always someone standing there waiting on me. But the work stuff is what made it impossible to ignore. Last week it happened enough that I got written up, and that kind of scared me, because I can’t really explain it as just being tired or distracted anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing repetitive things, like tapping or repeating actions, when you're alone or not at work?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s harder for me to say for sure. I don’t notice it in the same obvious way, because there isn’t a customer standing there waiting on me or a line forming, so there’s not that immediate pressure that makes it stand out.\n\nThe main place it’s really happening is at the register. I’ll be doing something normal, like scanning items, and then I get caught tapping the scanner in a pattern, like my hand just keeps doing that instead of moving on. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just freeze over the cash drawer, and I know the person is waiting, but I don’t move right away. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing in that moment.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It got bad enough last week that my manager wrote me up for it, and that made it feel like, okay, this isn’t just me being a little distracted or having a weird day. It’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes of getting stuck happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’d call it anxiety, at least not at the very start of it. It’s more like I suddenly realize I’m not moving forward, and then I become very aware that someone is waiting on me. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. I don’t know if I’m anxious and then I get stuck, or if I get stuck and then the anxiety comes because I can see the situation going wrong. Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? Just move your hand, just finish the transaction,” and that makes me feel more pressured.\n\nSo... yes, I feel anxious during it, but I don’t know if anxiety is causing it. It feels more like panic after I notice I’m stuck, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I’m doing it because I believe something bad will happen if I don’t, no. Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. I’m not sure. I mean, I do think about what happened afterward, like if something went wrong or if I embarrassed myself, I’ll replay it in my head for a while. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by thoughts that keep repeating.

It’s not like there’s one specific phrase or idea that keeps forcing itself into my mind over and over and I can’t get rid of it. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. Sometimes I’ll keep thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What did that customer think?” but that feels more like worrying about the situation after it happens than some separate repeated thought.

So... I guess I’d say not really, or I’m not aware of it. The getting stuck is more in what I’m doing than in a thought I can clearly point to.

That’s where it’s been obvious enough that other people notice, because there’s a customer right there and I’m supposed to be scanning or making change, and instead I’ll get caught tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer like I forgot how to move to the next step.\n\nOutside of work... I don’t know if I notice it the same way, maybe because there isn’t always someone standing there waiting on me. But the work stuff is what made it impossible to ignore. Last week it happened enough that I got written up, and that kind of scared me, because I can’t really explain it as just being tired or distracted anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing repetitive things, like tapping or repeating actions, when you're alone or not at work?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s harder for me to say for sure. I’ll be doing something normal, like scanning items, and then I get caught tapping the scanner in a pattern, like my hand just keeps doing that instead of moving on. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing in that moment.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It got bad enough last week that my manager wrote me up for it, and that made it feel like, okay, this isn’t just me being a little distracted or having a weird day. It’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes of getting stuck happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’d call it anxiety, at least not at the very start of it. It’s more like I suddenly realize I’m not moving forward, and then I become very aware that someone is waiting on me. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. I don’t know if I’m anxious and then I get stuck, or if I get stuck and then the anxiety comes because I can see the situation going wrong. Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it an overwhelming urge, exactly. That word “urge” makes it sound like there’s this strong feeling inside me saying, “You have to do this now,” and I’m not sure that’s what happens.

When I’m doing one of those things, it’s more like I’m already in it before I’ve really decided anything. I don’t usually have a clear thought like, “I need to repeat this,” or “I have to do it this way.” It’s not like I’m planning a ritual. It just kind of happens, and then once I notice it, I feel stuck in the middle of it and it’s hard to shift back to what I’m supposed to be doing.

I guess there can be a pull to keep doing whatever my hand is doing for a moment, but I don’t experience it as something meaningful or necessary, if that makes sense. It’s not satisfying, really. It doesn’t feel like relief afterward. Mostly I just feel confused or frustrated because I realize I’ve paused or repeated something and I don’t understand why.

So... no, I don’t think I have rituals I feel driven to do. At least not in the way I imagine you mean. It feels less like a ritual and more like I lose the thread of the action for a bit.

Sometimes I’ll keep thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What did that customer think?” but that feels more like worrying about the situation after it happens than some separate repeated thought.\n\nSo... I guess I’d say not really, or I’m not aware of it. The getting stuck is more in what I’m doing than in a thought I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it an overwhelming urge, exactly. That word “urge” makes it sound like there’s this strong feeling inside me saying, “You have to do this now,” and I’m not sure that’s what happens.\n\nWhen I’m doing one of those things, it’s more like I’m already in it before I’ve really decided anything. I don’t usually have a clear thought like, “I need to repeat this,” or “I have to do it this way.” It’s not like I’m planning a ritual. It just kind of happens, and then once I notice it, I feel stuck in the middle of it and it’s hard to shift back to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nI guess there can be a pull to keep doing whatever my hand is doing for a moment, but I don’t experience it as something meaningful or necessary, if that makes sense. It’s not satisfying, really. It doesn’t feel like relief afterward. Mostly I just feel confused or frustrated because I realize I’ve paused or repeated something and I don’t understand why.\n\nSo... no, I don’t think I have rituals I feel driven to do. At least not in the way I imagine you mean. It feels less like a ritual and more like I lose the thread of the action for a bit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really like twitching or blinking, no. I mean, I don’t think I have a tic where my eye keeps going or I make sounds without meaning to. At least nobody’s pointed that out to me.

What I do notice is more with my hands at work. Like I’ve said, I’ll be at the register and I’m supposed to be doing a normal thing—scanning, taking money, giving change—and then I get stuck in some movement that doesn’t match the situation. I’ll tap the scanner in this pattern, or I’ll just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting. It’s embarrassing because I know what I’m supposed to be doing, but for those moments I’m not doing it.

So I don’t know if that counts as an unusual movement. It’s not a sound or a blink, and it doesn’t feel like a twitch exactly. It’s more like my body gets caught doing the wrong thing, or stops halfway through the right thing. That’s the part that got me in trouble at work—last week they wrote me up for it because it looked like I was fooling around or not paying attention.

Last week it happened enough that I got written up, and that kind of scared me, because I can’t really explain it as just being tired or distracted anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing repetitive things, like tapping or repeating actions, when you're alone or not at work?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s harder for me to say for sure. I don’t notice it in the same obvious way, because there isn’t a customer standing there waiting on me or a line forming, so there’s not that immediate pressure that makes it stand out.\n\nThe main place it’s really happening is at the register. I’ll be doing something normal, like scanning items, and then I get caught tapping the scanner in a pattern, like my hand just keeps doing that instead of moving on. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just freeze over the cash drawer, and I know the person is waiting, but I don’t move right away. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing in that moment.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It got bad enough last week that my manager wrote me up for it, and that made it feel like, okay, this isn’t just me being a little distracted or having a weird day. It’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes of getting stuck happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’d call it anxiety, at least not at the very start of it. It’s more like I suddenly realize I’m not moving forward, and then I become very aware that someone is waiting on me. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. I don’t know if I’m anxious and then I get stuck, or if I get stuck and then the anxiety comes because I can see the situation going wrong. Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? Just move your hand, just finish the transaction,” and that makes me feel more pressured.\n\nSo... yes, I feel anxious during it, but I don’t know if anxiety is causing it. It feels more like panic after I notice I’m stuck, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I’m doing it because I believe something bad will happen if I don’t, no. It’s not like, “I have to tap the scanner three times or something terrible will happen.” At least, I don’t experience it that way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be at the register, in the middle of a normal transaction, and then I get stuck doing something that doesn’t really fit what’s going on. Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to make things feel “right” or prevent something. It’s more like my actions get stuck in the wrong place.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do think about what happened afterward, like if something went wrong or if I embarrassed myself, I’ll replay it in my head for a while. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by thoughts that keep repeating.\n\nIt’s not like there’s one specific phrase or idea that keeps forcing itself into my mind over and over and I can’t get rid of it. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. Sometimes I’ll keep thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What did that customer think?” but that feels more like worrying about the situation after it happens than some separate repeated thought.\n\nSo... I guess I’d say not really, or I’m not aware of it. The getting stuck is more in what I’m doing than in a thought I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it an overwhelming urge, exactly. That word “urge” makes it sound like there’s this strong feeling inside me saying, “You have to do this now,” and I’m not sure that’s what happens.\n\nWhen I’m doing one of those things, it’s more like I’m already in it before I’ve really decided anything. I don’t usually have a clear thought like, “I need to repeat this,” or “I have to do it this way.” It’s not like I’m planning a ritual. It just kind of happens, and then once I notice it, I feel stuck in the middle of it and it’s hard to shift back to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nI guess there can be a pull to keep doing whatever my hand is doing for a moment, but I don’t experience it as something meaningful or necessary, if that makes sense. It’s not satisfying, really. It doesn’t feel like relief afterward. Mostly I just feel confused or frustrated because I realize I’ve paused or repeated something and I don’t understand why.\n\nSo... no, I don’t think I have rituals I feel driven to do. At least not in the way I imagine you mean. It feels less like a ritual and more like I lose the thread of the action for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself making unusual sounds or movements, like twitching or blinking, that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Not really like twitching or blinking, no. I mean, I don’t think I have a tic where my eye keeps going or I make sounds without meaning to. At least nobody’s pointed that out to me.\n\nWhat I do notice is more with my hands at work. Like I’ve said, I’ll be at the register and I’m supposed to be doing a normal thing—scanning, taking money, giving change—and then I get stuck in some movement that doesn’t match the situation. I’ll tap the scanner in this pattern, or I’ll just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting. It’s embarrassing because I know what I’m supposed to be doing, but for those moments I’m not doing it.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as an unusual movement. It’s not a sound or a blink, and it doesn’t feel like a twitch exactly. It’s more like my body gets caught doing the wrong thing, or stops halfway through the right thing. That’s the part that got me in trouble at work—last week they wrote me up for it because it looked like I was fooling around or not paying attention.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F42", "F84.0", "G25.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

That’s the part that got me in trouble at work—last week they wrote me up for it because it looked like I was fooling around or not paying attention.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your symptoms have affected your ability to maintain relationships or perform daily tasks outside of work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m on a formal warning at work now, and outside of my shifts I’ve stopped keeping up with friends, kayaking plans, and family calls the way I used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes... I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first.

Work is the most obvious because there’s paperwork now. I’m on a formal warning, and that feels serious, like it’s not just me worrying that I’m slower or making people impatient. They actually wrote it down, and now I feel like every shift I’m being watched or like I have to prove I can get through basic tasks without messing them up. That’s been hard.

Outside of work, it’s more gradual, but it’s there. I used to keep up with people better. I’d call family, answer messages, make plans with friends. I used to go kayaking when people invited me, or I’d be the one trying to set it up sometimes. Lately I just... don’t. I let texts sit there. I tell myself I’ll call back after dinner, then it’s the next day, then it feels awkward because I waited too long. Same with kayaking plans — I’ll think maybe I should go, it would probably be good for me, but then I don’t follow through.

So daily tasks outside work aren’t completely impossible, but I’m not functioning the way I used to. I’m more behind on keeping contact with people, and I think some of them have stopped asking as much because I haven’t been reliable. That part bothers me.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F84.0, G25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because things have been getting... strange at work, I guess. I’m a cashier, and lately when I’m at the register I keep getting stuck doing things that don’t really fit what’s happening. Like I’ll be scanning items and then I’ll start tapping the scanner in a certain pattern, not because I need to, just... I get caught in it. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just sort of freeze over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting and staring at me.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to just keep moving, ring them up, hand them the receipt, all of that. I’ve done this job long enough. But in the moment I get stuck, like my body is doing the wrong thing or not doing the next thing. Last week it happened enough that my manager wrote me up, and that’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone instead of just trying to pretend it’s not happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these episodes of getting stuck are affecting your interactions with customers or coworkers outside of just the immediate task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, it has. It’s not just that one awkward moment with the customer waiting, although that’s bad enough. It sort of spills over. At work people have started treating me like I’m unreliable, or like they have to watch me, and I can feel that. My manager put me on a formal warning now, so it’s not just in my head that it’s a problem there. It’s on paper.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been pulling back more than I want to admit. I used to keep up with friends better, make kayaking plans when the weather was decent, call my family regularly. Lately I’ll see a message or think, “I should call,” and then I just don’t. Or I put it off until it feels too late to respond normally. So it’s affecting people around me, yeah. I’m not keeping up with them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these patterns of getting stuck or doing things in a certain repetitive way when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, the clearest place it happens is at work, at the register. That’s where it’s been obvious enough that other people notice, because there’s a customer right there and I’m supposed to be scanning or making change, and instead I’ll get caught tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer like I forgot how to move to the next step.\n\nOutside of work... I don’t know if I notice it the same way, maybe because there isn’t always someone standing there waiting on me. But the work stuff is what made it impossible to ignore. Last week it happened enough that I got written up, and that kind of scared me, because I can’t really explain it as just being tired or distracted anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing repetitive things, like tapping or repeating actions, when you're alone or not at work?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s harder for me to say for sure. I don’t notice it in the same obvious way, because there isn’t a customer standing there waiting on me or a line forming, so there’s not that immediate pressure that makes it stand out.\n\nThe main place it’s really happening is at the register. I’ll be doing something normal, like scanning items, and then I get caught tapping the scanner in a pattern, like my hand just keeps doing that instead of moving on. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just freeze over the cash drawer, and I know the person is waiting, but I don’t move right away. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing in that moment.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It got bad enough last week that my manager wrote me up for it, and that made it feel like, okay, this isn’t just me being a little distracted or having a weird day. It’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes of getting stuck happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’d call it anxiety, at least not at the very start of it. It’s more like I suddenly realize I’m not moving forward, and then I become very aware that someone is waiting on me. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. I don’t know if I’m anxious and then I get stuck, or if I get stuck and then the anxiety comes because I can see the situation going wrong. Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? Just move your hand, just finish the transaction,” and that makes me feel more pressured.\n\nSo... yes, I feel anxious during it, but I don’t know if anxiety is causing it. It feels more like panic after I notice I’m stuck, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I’m doing it because I believe something bad will happen if I don’t, no. It’s not like, “I have to tap the scanner three times or something terrible will happen.” At least, I don’t experience it that way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be at the register, in the middle of a normal transaction, and then I get stuck doing something that doesn’t really fit what’s going on. Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to make things feel “right” or prevent something. It’s more like my actions get stuck in the wrong place.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do think about what happened afterward, like if something went wrong or if I embarrassed myself, I’ll replay it in my head for a while. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by thoughts that keep repeating.\n\nIt’s not like there’s one specific phrase or idea that keeps forcing itself into my mind over and over and I can’t get rid of it. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. Sometimes I’ll keep thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What did that customer think?” but that feels more like worrying about the situation after it happens than some separate repeated thought.\n\nSo... I guess I’d say not really, or I’m not aware of it. The getting stuck is more in what I’m doing than in a thought I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it an overwhelming urge, exactly. That word “urge” makes it sound like there’s this strong feeling inside me saying, “You have to do this now,” and I’m not sure that’s what happens.\n\nWhen I’m doing one of those things, it’s more like I’m already in it before I’ve really decided anything. I don’t usually have a clear thought like, “I need to repeat this,” or “I have to do it this way.” It’s not like I’m planning a ritual. It just kind of happens, and then once I notice it, I feel stuck in the middle of it and it’s hard to shift back to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nI guess there can be a pull to keep doing whatever my hand is doing for a moment, but I don’t experience it as something meaningful or necessary, if that makes sense. It’s not satisfying, really. It doesn’t feel like relief afterward. Mostly I just feel confused or frustrated because I realize I’ve paused or repeated something and I don’t understand why.\n\nSo... no, I don’t think I have rituals I feel driven to do. At least not in the way I imagine you mean. It feels less like a ritual and more like I lose the thread of the action for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself making unusual sounds or movements, like twitching or blinking, that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Not really like twitching or blinking, no. I mean, I don’t think I have a tic where my eye keeps going or I make sounds without meaning to. At least nobody’s pointed that out to me.\n\nWhat I do notice is more with my hands at work. Like I’ve said, I’ll be at the register and I’m supposed to be doing a normal thing—scanning, taking money, giving change—and then I get stuck in some movement that doesn’t match the situation. I’ll tap the scanner in this pattern, or I’ll just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting. It’s embarrassing because I know what I’m supposed to be doing, but for those moments I’m not doing it.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as an unusual movement. It’s not a sound or a blink, and it doesn’t feel like a twitch exactly. It’s more like my body gets caught doing the wrong thing, or stops halfway through the right thing. That’s the part that got me in trouble at work—last week they wrote me up for it because it looked like I was fooling around or not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms have affected your ability to maintain relationships or perform daily tasks outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes... I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork is the most obvious because there’s paperwork now. I’m on a formal warning, and that feels serious, like it’s not just me worrying that I’m slower or making people impatient. That’s been hard.\n\nOutside of work, it’s more gradual, but it’s there. I used to keep up with people better. I’d call family, answer messages, make plans with friends. That part bothers me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Schizophrenia. functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m on a formal warning at work now, and outside of my shifts I’ve stopped keeping up with friends, months, since my youngest left the Eastern Shore for college.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the more clearly out-of-place moments have been happening for about the last three months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / cashier / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S022\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it an overwhelming urge, exactly. That word “urge” makes it sound like there’s this strong feeling inside me saying, “You have to do this now,” and I’m not sure that’s what happens.\n\nWhen I’m doing one of those things, it’s more like I’m already in it before I’ve really decided anything. I don’t usually have a clear thought like, “I need to repeat this,” or “I have to do it this way.” It’s not like I’m planning a ritual. It just kind of happens, and then once I notice it, I feel stuck in the middle of it and it’s hard to shift back to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nI guess there can be a pull to keep doing whatever my hand is doing for a moment, but I don’t experience it as something meaningful or necessary, if that makes sense. It’s not satisfying, really. It doesn’t feel like relief afterward. Mostly I just feel confused or frustrated because I realize I’ve paused or repeated something and I don’t understand why.\n\nSo... no, I don’t think I have rituals I feel driven to do. At least not in the way I imagine you mean. It feels less like a ritual and more like I lose the thread of the action for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself making unusual sounds or movements, like twitching or blinking, that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Not really like twitching or blinking, no. I mean, I don’t think I have a tic where my eye keeps going or I make sounds without meaning to. At least nobody’s pointed that out to me.\n\nWhat I do notice is more with my hands at work. Like I’ve said, I’ll be at the register and I’m supposed to be doing a normal thing—scanning, taking money, giving change—and then I get stuck in some movement that doesn’t match the situation. I’ll tap the scanner in this pattern, or I’ll just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting. It’s embarrassing because I know what I’m supposed to be doing, but for those moments I’m not doing it.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as an unusual movement. It’s not a sound or a blink, and it doesn’t feel like a twitch exactly. It’s more like my body gets caught doing the wrong thing, or stops halfway through the right thing. That’s the part that got me in trouble at work—last week they wrote me up for it because it looked like I was fooling around or not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms have affected your ability to maintain relationships or perform daily tasks outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes... I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork is the most obvious because there’s paperwork now. I’m on a formal warning, and that feels serious, like it’s not just me worrying that I’m slower or making people impatient. They actually wrote it down, and now I feel like every shift I’m being watched or like I have to prove I can get through basic tasks without messing them up. That’s been hard.\n\nOutside of work, it’s more gradual, but it’s there. I used to keep up with people better. I’d call family, answer messages, make plans with friends. I used to go kayaking when people invited me, or I’d be the one trying to set it up sometimes. Lately I just... don’t. I let texts sit there. I tell myself I’ll call back after dinner, then it’s the next day, then it feels awkward because I waited too long. Same with kayaking plans — I’ll think maybe I should go, it would probably be good for me, but then I don’t follow through.\n\nSo daily tasks outside work aren’t completely impossible, but I’m not functioning the way I used to. I’m more behind on keeping contact with people, and I think some of them have stopped asking as much because I haven’t been reliable. That part bothers me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your symptoms have made it harder for you to interact with friends and family?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it has. I mean, I was sort of trying to separate work from the rest of my life in my head, like, “Okay, the register problem is just at work,” but that’s not really true anymore.

I’m on a formal warning at work now, which has made me more tense in general, and then when I’m not working I’ve kind of stopped doing the normal keeping-up-with-people things. I don’t call family the way I used to. I’ll see a message and think I’m going to answer it later, and then I just don’t, or I wait so long that it feels embarrassing to respond. It’s not that I don’t care about them. That’s the part I feel bad about. I do care, but I’m not following through, and I think people notice after a while. So yes, interacting with friends and family has definitely gotten harder, mostly because I’m not keeping up with them like I used to.

Like I’ll be scanning items and then I’ll start tapping the scanner in a certain pattern, not because I need to, just... I get caught in it. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just sort of freeze over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting and staring at me.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to just keep moving, ring them up, hand them the receipt, all of that. I’ve done this job long enough. But in the moment I get stuck, like my body is doing the wrong thing or not doing the next thing. Last week it happened enough that my manager wrote me up, and that’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone instead of just trying to pretend it’s not happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these episodes of getting stuck are affecting your interactions with customers or coworkers outside of just the immediate task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, it has. It’s not just that one awkward moment with the customer waiting, although that’s bad enough. It sort of spills over. At work people have started treating me like I’m unreliable, or like they have to watch me, and I can feel that. My manager put me on a formal warning now, so it’s not just in my head that it’s a problem there. It’s on paper.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been pulling back more than I want to admit. I used to keep up with friends better, make kayaking plans when the weather was decent, call my family regularly. Lately I’ll see a message or think, “I should call,” and then I just don’t. Or I put it off until it feels too late to respond normally. So it’s affecting people around me, yeah. I’m not keeping up with them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these patterns of getting stuck or doing things in a certain repetitive way when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, the clearest place it happens is at work, at the register. That’s where it’s been obvious enough that other people notice, because there’s a customer right there and I’m supposed to be scanning or making change, and instead I’ll get caught tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer like I forgot how to move to the next step.\n\nOutside of work... I don’t know if I notice it the same way, maybe because there isn’t always someone standing there waiting on me. But the work stuff is what made it impossible to ignore. Last week it happened enough that I got written up, and that kind of scared me, because I can’t really explain it as just being tired or distracted anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing repetitive things, like tapping or repeating actions, when you're alone or not at work?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s harder for me to say for sure. I don’t notice it in the same obvious way, because there isn’t a customer standing there waiting on me or a line forming, so there’s not that immediate pressure that makes it stand out.\n\nThe main place it’s really happening is at the register. I’ll be doing something normal, like scanning items, and then I get caught tapping the scanner in a pattern, like my hand just keeps doing that instead of moving on. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just freeze over the cash drawer, and I know the person is waiting, but I don’t move right away. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing in that moment.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It got bad enough last week that my manager wrote me up for it, and that made it feel like, okay, this isn’t just me being a little distracted or having a weird day. It’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes of getting stuck happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’d call it anxiety, at least not at the very start of it. It’s more like I suddenly realize I’m not moving forward, and then I become very aware that someone is waiting on me. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. I don’t know if I’m anxious and then I get stuck, or if I get stuck and then the anxiety comes because I can see the situation going wrong. Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? Just move your hand, just finish the transaction,” and that makes me feel more pressured.\n\nSo... yes, I feel anxious during it, but I don’t know if anxiety is causing it. It feels more like panic after I notice I’m stuck, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I’m doing it because I believe something bad will happen if I don’t, no. It’s not like, “I have to tap the scanner three times or something terrible will happen.” At least, I don’t experience it that way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be at the register, in the middle of a normal transaction, and then I get stuck doing something that doesn’t really fit what’s going on. Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to make things feel “right” or prevent something. It’s more like my actions get stuck in the wrong place.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. Sometimes I’ll keep thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What did that customer think?” but that feels more like worrying about the situation after it happens than some separate repeated thought.\n\nSo... I don’t notice it in the same obvious way, because there isn’t a customer standing there waiting on me or a line forming, so there’s not that immediate pressure that makes it stand out.\n\nThe main place it’s really happening is at the register. I’ll be doing something normal, like scanning items, and then I get caught tapping the scanner in a pattern, like my hand just keeps doing that instead of moving on. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just freeze over the cash drawer, and I know the person is waiting, but I don’t move right away. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing in that moment.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It got bad enough last week that my manager wrote me up for it, and that made it feel like, okay, this isn’t just me being a little distracted or having a weird day. It’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes of getting stuck happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’d call it anxiety, at least not at the very start of it. It’s more like I suddenly realize I’m not moving forward, and then I become very aware that someone is waiting on me. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. I don’t know if I’m anxious and then I get stuck, or if I get stuck and then the anxiety comes because I can see the situation going wrong. Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? Just move your hand, just finish the transaction,” and that makes me feel more pressured.\n\nSo... yes, I feel anxious during it, but I don’t know if anxiety is causing it. It feels more like panic after I notice I’m stuck, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I’m doing it because I believe something bad will happen if I don’t, no. It’s not like, “I have to tap the scanner three times or something terrible will happen.” At least, I don’t experience it that way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be at the register, in the middle of a normal transaction, and then I get stuck doing something that doesn’t really fit what’s going on. Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to make things feel “right” or prevent something. It’s more like my actions get stuck in the wrong place.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do think about what happened afterward, like if something went wrong or if I embarrassed myself, I’ll replay it in my head for a while. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by thoughts that keep repeating.\n\nIt’s not like there’s one specific phrase or idea that keeps forcing itself into my mind over and over and I can’t get rid of it. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. Sometimes I’ll keep thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What did that customer think?” but that feels more like worrying about the situation after it happens than some separate repeated thought.\n\nSo... I guess I’d say not really, or I’m not aware of it. The getting stuck is more in what I’m doing than in a thought I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it an overwhelming urge, exactly. That word “urge” makes it sound like there’s this strong feeling inside me saying, “You have to do this now,” and I’m not sure that’s what happens.\n\nWhen I’m doing one of those things, it’s more like I’m already in it before I’ve really decided anything. I don’t usually have a clear thought like, “I need to repeat this,” or “I have to do it this way.” It’s not like I’m planning a ritual. It just kind of happens, and then once I notice it, I feel stuck in the middle of it and it’s hard to shift back to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nI guess there can be a pull to keep doing whatever my hand is doing for a moment, but I don’t experience it as something meaningful or necessary, if that makes sense. It’s not satisfying, really. It doesn’t feel like relief afterward. Mostly I just feel confused or frustrated because I realize I’ve paused or repeated something and I don’t understand why.\n\nSo... no, I don’t think I have rituals I feel driven to do. At least not in the way I imagine you mean. It feels less like a ritual and more like I lose the thread of the action for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself making unusual sounds or movements, like twitching or blinking, that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Not really like twitching or blinking, no. I mean, I don’t think I have a tic where my eye keeps going or I make sounds without meaning to. At least nobody’s pointed that out to me.\n\nWhat I do notice is more with my hands at work. Like I’ve said, I’ll be at the register and I’m supposed to be doing a normal thing—scanning, taking money, giving change—and then I get stuck in some movement that doesn’t match the situation. I’ll tap the scanner in this pattern, or I’ll just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting. It’s embarrassing because I know what I’m supposed to be doing, but for those moments I’m not doing it.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as an unusual movement. It’s not a sound or a blink, and it doesn’t feel like a twitch exactly. It’s more like my body gets caught doing the wrong thing, or stops halfway through the right thing. That’s the part that got me in trouble at work—last week they wrote me up for it because it looked like I was fooling around or not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms have affected your ability to maintain relationships or perform daily tasks outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes... I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork is the most obvious because there’s paperwork now. I’m on a formal warning, and that feels serious, like it’s not just me worrying that I’m slower or making people impatient. They actually wrote it down, and now I feel like every shift I’m being watched or like I have to prove I can get through basic tasks without messing them up. That’s been hard.\n\nOutside of work, it’s more gradual, but it’s there. I used to keep up with people better. I’d call family, answer messages, make plans with friends. I used to go kayaking when people invited me, or I’d be the one trying to set it up sometimes. Lately I just... don’t. I let texts sit there. I tell myself I’ll call back after dinner, then it’s the next day, then it feels awkward because I waited too long. Same with kayaking plans — I’ll think maybe I should go, it would probably be good for me, but then I don’t follow through.\n\nSo daily tasks outside work aren’t completely impossible, but I’m not functioning the way I used to. I’m more behind on keeping contact with people, and I think some of them have stopped asking as much because I haven’t been reliable. That part bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms have made it harder for you to interact with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I was sort of trying to separate work from the rest of my life in my head, like, “Okay, the register problem is just at work,” but that’s not really true anymore.\n\nI’m on a formal warning at work now, which has made me more tense in general, and then when I’m not working I’ve kind of stopped doing the normal keeping-up-with-people things. I don’t call family the way I used to. I’ll see a message and think I’m going to answer it later, and then I just don’t, or I wait so long that it feels embarrassing to respond. And with friends, especially the kayaking plans, I used to be more involved. I’d go when people asked, or help figure out a day, and now I just let it pass by.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about them. That’s the part I feel bad about. I do care, but I’m not following through, and I think people notice after a while. So yes, interacting with friends and family has definitely gotten harder, mostly because I’m not keeping up with them like I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. Like, outside of work I don’t think I have a thing where I have to touch the doorknob five times or check something over and over until it feels “right,” if that’s what you mean.

Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? Just move your hand, just finish the transaction,” and that makes me feel more pressured.\n\nSo... yes, I feel anxious during it, but I don’t know if anxiety is causing it. It feels more like panic after I notice I’m stuck, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I’m doing it because I believe something bad will happen if I don’t, no. It’s not like, “I have to tap the scanner three times or something terrible will happen.” At least, I don’t experience it that way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be at the register, in the middle of a normal transaction, and then I get stuck doing something that doesn’t really fit what’s going on. Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to make things feel “right” or prevent something. It’s more like my actions get stuck in the wrong place.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do think about what happened afterward, like if something went wrong or if I embarrassed myself, I’ll replay it in my head for a while. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by thoughts that keep repeating.\n\nIt’s not like there’s one specific phrase or idea that keeps forcing itself into my mind over and over and I can’t get rid of it. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. Sometimes I’ll keep thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What did that customer think?” but that feels more like worrying about the situation after it happens than some separate repeated thought.\n\nSo... I guess I’d say not really, or I’m not aware of it. The getting stuck is more in what I’m doing than in a thought I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it an overwhelming urge, exactly. That word “urge” makes it sound like there’s this strong feeling inside me saying, “You have to do this now,” and I’m not sure that’s what happens.\n\nWhen I’m doing one of those things, it’s more like I’m already in it before I’ve really decided anything. I don’t usually have a clear thought like, “I need to repeat this,” or “I have to do it this way.” It’s not like I’m planning a ritual. It just kind of happens, and then once I notice it, I feel stuck in the middle of it and it’s hard to shift back to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nI guess there can be a pull to keep doing whatever my hand is doing for a moment, but I don’t experience it as something meaningful or necessary, if that makes sense. It’s not satisfying, really. It doesn’t feel like relief afterward. Mostly I just feel confused or frustrated because I realize I’ve paused or repeated something and I don’t understand why.\n\nSo... no, I don’t think I have rituals I feel driven to do. At least not in the way I imagine you mean. It feels less like a ritual and more like I lose the thread of the action for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself making unusual sounds or movements, like twitching or blinking, that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Not really like twitching or blinking, no. I mean, I don’t think I have a tic where my eye keeps going or I make sounds without meaning to. At least nobody’s pointed that out to me.\n\nWhat I do notice is more with my hands at work. Like I’ve said, I’ll be at the register and I’m supposed to be doing a normal thing—scanning, taking money, giving change—and then I get stuck in some movement that doesn’t match the situation. I’ll tap the scanner in this pattern, or I’ll just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting. It’s embarrassing because I know what I’m supposed to be doing, but for those moments I’m not doing it.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as an unusual movement. It’s not a sound or a blink, and it doesn’t feel like a twitch exactly. It’s more like my body gets caught doing the wrong thing, or stops halfway through the right thing. That’s the part that got me in trouble at work—last week they wrote me up for it because it looked like I was fooling around or not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms have affected your ability to maintain relationships or perform daily tasks outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes... I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork is the most obvious because there’s paperwork now. I’m on a formal warning, and that feels serious, like it’s not just me worrying that I’m slower or making people impatient. They actually wrote it down, and now I feel like every shift I’m being watched or like I have to prove I can get through basic tasks without messing them up. That’s been hard.\n\nOutside of work, it’s more gradual, but it’s there. I used to keep up with people better. I’d call family, answer messages, make plans with friends. I used to go kayaking when people invited me, or I’d be the one trying to set it up sometimes. Lately I just... don’t. I let texts sit there. I tell myself I’ll call back after dinner, then it’s the next day, then it feels awkward because I waited too long. Same with kayaking plans — I’ll think maybe I should go, it would probably be good for me, but then I don’t follow through.\n\nSo daily tasks outside work aren’t completely impossible, but I’m not functioning the way I used to. I’m more behind on keeping contact with people, and I think some of them have stopped asking as much because I haven’t been reliable. That part bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms have made it harder for you to interact with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I was sort of trying to separate work from the rest of my life in my head, like, “Okay, the register problem is just at work,” but that’s not really true anymore.\n\nI’m on a formal warning at work now, which has made me more tense in general, and then when I’m not working I’ve kind of stopped doing the normal keeping-up-with-people things. I don’t call family the way I used to. I’ll see a message and think I’m going to answer it later, and then I just don’t, or I wait so long that it feels embarrassing to respond. And with friends, especially the kayaking plans, I used to be more involved. I’d go when people asked, or help figure out a day, and now I just let it pass by.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about them. That’s the part I feel bad about. I do care, but I’m not following through, and I think people notice after a while. So yes, interacting with friends and family has definitely gotten harder, mostly because I’m not keeping up with them like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors outside of work to feel a sense of completion or relief?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. Like, outside of work I don’t think I have a thing where I have to touch the doorknob five times or check something over and over until it feels “right,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI do sometimes get stuck trying to finish things, but it’s more like I lose the thread or I sit there longer than I should, not that I’m repeating a behavior for relief. I don’t really get that feeling of, “If I do this action again, then I’ll feel complete.” At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is probably no, or not in a clear way. I may need you to give me an example if there’s a specific kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
