It’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes of getting stuck happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’d call it anxiety, at least not at the very start of it. It’s more like I suddenly realize I’m not moving forward, and then I become very aware that someone is waiting on me. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. I don’t know if I’m anxious and then I get stuck, or if I get stuck and then the anxiety comes because I can see the situation going wrong. Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? Just move your hand, just finish the transaction,” and that makes me feel more pressured.\n\nSo... yes, I feel anxious during it, but I don’t know if anxiety is causing it. It feels more like panic after I notice I’m stuck, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I’m doing it because I believe something bad will happen if I don’t, no. It’s not like, “I have to tap the scanner three times or something terrible will happen.” At least, I don’t experience it that way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be at the register, in the middle of a normal transaction, and then I get stuck doing something that doesn’t really fit what’s going on. Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to make things feel “right” or prevent something. It’s more like my actions get stuck in the wrong place.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do think about what happened afterward, like if something went wrong or if I embarrassed myself, I’ll replay it in my head for a while. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by thoughts that keep repeating.\n\nIt’s not like there’s one specific phrase or idea that keeps forcing itself into my mind over and over and I can’t get rid of it. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. Sometimes I’ll keep thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What did that customer think?” but that feels more like worrying about the situation after it happens than some separate repeated thought.\n\nSo... I guess I’d say not really, or I’m not aware of it. The getting stuck is more in what I’m doing than in a thought I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it an overwhelming urge, exactly. That word “urge” makes it sound like there’s this strong feeling inside me saying, “You have to do this now,” and I’m not sure that’s what happens.\n\nWhen I’m doing one of those things, it’s more like I’m already in it before I’ve really decided anything. I don’t usually have a clear thought like, “I need to repeat this,” or “I have to do it this way.” It’s not like I’m planning a ritual. It just kind of happens, and then once I notice it, I feel stuck in the middle of it and it’s hard to shift back to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nI guess there can be a pull to keep doing whatever my hand is doing for a moment, but I don’t experience it as something meaningful or necessary, if that makes sense. It’s not satisfying, really. It doesn’t feel like relief afterward. Mostly I just feel confused or frustrated because I realize I’ve paused or repeated something and I don’t understand why.\n\nSo... no, I don’t think I have rituals I feel driven to do. At least not in the way I imagine you mean. It feels less like a ritual and more like I lose the thread of the action for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself making unusual sounds or movements, like twitching or blinking, that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Not really like twitching or blinking, no. I mean, I don’t think I have a tic where my eye keeps going or I make sounds without meaning to. At least nobody’s pointed that out to me.\n\nWhat I do notice is more with my hands at work. Like I’ve said, I’ll be at the register and I’m supposed to be doing a normal thing—scanning, taking money, giving change—and then I get stuck in some movement that doesn’t match the situation. I’ll tap the scanner in this pattern, or I’ll just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting. It’s embarrassing because I know what I’m supposed to be doing, but for those moments I’m not doing it.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as an unusual movement. It’s not a sound or a blink, and it doesn’t feel like a twitch exactly. It’s more like my body gets caught doing the wrong thing, or stops halfway through the right thing. That’s the part that got me in trouble at work—last week they wrote me up for it because it looked like I was fooling around or not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms have affected your ability to maintain relationships or perform daily tasks outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes... I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork is the most obvious because there’s paperwork now. I’m on a formal warning, and that feels serious, like it’s not just me worrying that I’m slower or making people impatient. They actually wrote it down, and now I feel like every shift I’m being watched or like I have to prove I can get through basic tasks without messing them up. That’s been hard.\n\nOutside of work, it’s more gradual, but it’s there. I used to keep up with people better. I’d call family, answer messages, make plans with friends. I used to go kayaking when people invited me, or I’d be the one trying to set it up sometimes. Lately I just... don’t. I let texts sit there. I tell myself I’ll call back after dinner, then it’s the next day, then it feels awkward because I waited too long. Same with kayaking plans — I’ll think maybe I should go, it would probably be good for me, but then I don’t follow through.\n\nSo daily tasks outside work aren’t completely impossible, but I’m not functioning the way I used to. I’m more behind on keeping contact with people, and I think some of them have stopped asking as much because I haven’t been reliable. That part bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms have made it harder for you to interact with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I was sort of trying to separate work from the rest of my life in my head, like, “Okay, the register problem is just at work,” but that’s not really true anymore.\n\nI’m on a formal warning at work now, which has made me more tense in general, and then when I’m not working I’ve kind of stopped doing the normal keeping-up-with-people things. I don’t call family the way I used to. I’ll see a message and think I’m going to answer it later, and then I just don’t, or I wait so long that it feels embarrassing to respond. And with friends, especially the kayaking plans, I used to be more involved. I’d go when people asked, or help figure out a day, and now I just let it pass by.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about them. That’s the part I feel bad about. I do care, but I’m not following through, and I think people notice after a while. So yes, interacting with friends and family has definitely gotten harder, mostly because I’m not keeping up with them like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors outside of work to feel a sense of completion or relief?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. Like, outside of work I don’t think I have a thing where I have to touch the doorknob five times or check something over and over until it feels “right,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI do sometimes get stuck trying to finish things, but it’s more like I lose the thread or I sit there longer than I should, not that I’m repeating a behavior for relief. I don’t really get that feeling of, “If I do this action again, then I’ll feel complete.” At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is probably no, or not in a clear way. I may need you to give me an example if there’s a specific kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, everybody has things pop into their head, right? Like I’ll be trying to read something or watch TV and then I remember I didn’t answer someone’s message, or I start thinking about whether I handled something badly earlier. But I wouldn’t say there are specific images or thoughts that keep forcing their way in over and over no matter what I do.\n\nSometimes my mind feels busy, or I have a hard time staying with one thing, but it’s not like there’s one disturbing picture or one particular phrase that keeps coming back and I can’t get rid of it. At least… not that I’ve noticed as a pattern.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, or maybe I’m not totally sure what counts. If you mean unwanted thoughts that repeat and feel intrusive, I don’t think that’s really been happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring mental images or thoughts that you feel compelled to think about when you're getting stuck in these repetitive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, when you say “compelled to think about,” I don’t really recognize that feeling in myself.\n\nI can get stuck, sure, and sometimes I’ll sit there kind of circling around something in my head longer than I probably should. But it’s not like there’s a specific image or thought that I have to bring up in my mind to make the behavior feel finished, if that makes sense. I’m not thinking, “Okay, I need to picture this,” or “I need to repeat this thought until it feels right.”\n\nMostly it’s more vague than that. Like I might be aware I’m not moving on from something, or I’m taking too long, but I’m not aware of a recurring mental picture or phrase driving it. If there is something happening, I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to describe it. So I’d say probably no, not in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is affecting my life overall. I don’t always like admitting that, because part of me wants to say I’m still managing, I still go to work, I still do what I have to do. But if I’m being honest, things have slipped.\n\nAt work, it’s become serious enough that I’m on a formal warning now. That’s not something I’ve ever wanted attached to my name, and it’s been weighing on me. I try to tell myself, you know, “Just get through the shift, keep your head down, do the job,” but clearly it’s not invisible to other people anymore.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve really pulled back. I used to keep up with friends more. I used to make kayaking plans, or at least be the kind of person who would say yes when someone suggested it. I’d call family more regularly too. Now I keep putting things off, or I don’t respond, or I tell myself I’ll call tomorrow and then it becomes next week. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I just haven’t been keeping up with people or with the things that made my life feel normal.\n\nSo yes, I’d say my quality of life has definitely taken a hit. Not completely collapsed, but… it’s smaller than it used to be, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any unwanted thoughts that keep coming into your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way. I know I sort of answered something like this already, but I’m trying to think if there’s another way to say it.\n\nI do have thoughts come and go, and sometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about things longer than I want to, but I wouldn’t say there are specific unwanted thoughts that keep barging in and that I’m fighting off all day. It’s more like normal worries or random things I remember, and then sometimes I have trouble shifting my attention away from them. But they don’t feel like these repeated intrusive thoughts that I can’t control, if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, if I’m distracted, I might keep turning something over in my head, but it’s not a particular thought that keeps coming back against my will in the same way every time. I haven’t noticed that pattern. So my answer would be… probably no. Or at least, not something I can identify clearly as that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I’d say they are, at this point. I mean, I can still get through parts of my day, so sometimes I catch myself wanting to downplay it, like, “Well, I’m still showing up, I’m still doing the basics.” But the truth is, it’s interfering enough that other people are noticing.

Work is the clearest example. I’m a cashier, and I’m on a formal warning now. That’s been hard to sit with, because I don’t like feeling like I’m becoming unreliable or like I’m making problems for the people around me. It makes each shift feel heavier, because I’m aware I can’t just keep saying it’s only in my head or only affecting me.

And then outside of work, it’s like my life has narrowed. I’ve stopped keeping up with friends the way I used to. Kayaking plans, which used to be something I actually looked forward to, have just kind of fallen away. Family calls too — I’ll think, “I should call,” and then I don’t, and then more time passes, and then it feels awkward, and I put it off again.

So yes, it’s definitely affecting my functioning and my ability to enjoy things. Not every single minute, but enough that my daily life doesn’t feel like it used to.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F84.0, G25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because things have been getting... strange at work, I guess. I’m a cashier, and lately when I’m at the register I keep getting stuck doing things that don’t really fit what’s happening. Like I’ll be scanning items and then I’ll start tapping the scanner in a certain pattern, not because I need to, just... I get caught in it. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just sort of freeze over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting and staring at me.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to just keep moving, ring them up, hand them the receipt, all of that. I’ve done this job long enough. But in the moment I get stuck, like my body is doing the wrong thing or not doing the next thing. Last week it happened enough that my manager wrote me up, and that’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone instead of just trying to pretend it’s not happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these episodes of getting stuck are affecting your interactions with customers or coworkers outside of just the immediate task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, it has. It’s not just that one awkward moment with the customer waiting, although that’s bad enough. It sort of spills over. At work people have started treating me like I’m unreliable, or like they have to watch me, and I can feel that. My manager put me on a formal warning now, so it’s not just in my head that it’s a problem there. It’s on paper.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been pulling back more than I want to admit. I used to keep up with friends better, make kayaking plans when the weather was decent, call my family regularly. Lately I’ll see a message or think, “I should call,” and then I just don’t. Or I put it off until it feels too late to respond normally. So it’s affecting people around me, yeah. I’m not keeping up with them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these patterns of getting stuck or doing things in a certain repetitive way when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, the clearest place it happens is at work, at the register. That’s where it’s been obvious enough that other people notice, because there’s a customer right there and I’m supposed to be scanning or making change, and instead I’ll get caught tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer like I forgot how to move to the next step.\n\nOutside of work... I don’t know if I notice it the same way, maybe because there isn’t always someone standing there waiting on me. But the work stuff is what made it impossible to ignore. Last week it happened enough that I got written up, and that kind of scared me, because I can’t really explain it as just being tired or distracted anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing repetitive things, like tapping or repeating actions, when you're alone or not at work?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone, it’s harder for me to say for sure. I don’t notice it in the same obvious way, because there isn’t a customer standing there waiting on me or a line forming, so there’s not that immediate pressure that makes it stand out.\n\nThe main place it’s really happening is at the register. I’ll be doing something normal, like scanning items, and then I get caught tapping the scanner in a pattern, like my hand just keeps doing that instead of moving on. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just freeze over the cash drawer, and I know the person is waiting, but I don’t move right away. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing in that moment.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It got bad enough last week that my manager wrote me up for it, and that made it feel like, okay, this isn’t just me being a little distracted or having a weird day. It’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes of getting stuck happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’d call it anxiety, at least not at the very start of it. It’s more like I suddenly realize I’m not moving forward, and then I become very aware that someone is waiting on me. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. I don’t know if I’m anxious and then I get stuck, or if I get stuck and then the anxiety comes because I can see the situation going wrong. Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? Just move your hand, just finish the transaction,” and that makes me feel more pressured.\n\nSo... yes, I feel anxious during it, but I don’t know if anxiety is causing it. It feels more like panic after I notice I’m stuck, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I’m doing it because I believe something bad will happen if I don’t, no. It’s not like, “I have to tap the scanner three times or something terrible will happen.” At least, I don’t experience it that way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be at the register, in the middle of a normal transaction, and then I get stuck doing something that doesn’t really fit what’s going on. Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to make things feel “right” or prevent something. It’s more like my actions get stuck in the wrong place.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to get rid of?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do think about what happened afterward, like if something went wrong or if I embarrassed myself, I’ll replay it in my head for a while. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by thoughts that keep repeating.\n\nIt’s not like there’s one specific phrase or idea that keeps forcing itself into my mind over and over and I can’t get rid of it. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. Sometimes I’ll keep thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What did that customer think?” but that feels more like worrying about the situation after it happens than some separate repeated thought.\n\nSo... I guess I’d say not really, or I’m not aware of it. The getting stuck is more in what I’m doing than in a thought I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it an overwhelming urge, exactly. That word “urge” makes it sound like there’s this strong feeling inside me saying, “You have to do this now,” and I’m not sure that’s what happens.\n\nWhen I’m doing one of those things, it’s more like I’m already in it before I’ve really decided anything. I don’t usually have a clear thought like, “I need to repeat this,” or “I have to do it this way.” It’s not like I’m planning a ritual. It just kind of happens, and then once I notice it, I feel stuck in the middle of it and it’s hard to shift back to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nI guess there can be a pull to keep doing whatever my hand is doing for a moment, but I don’t experience it as something meaningful or necessary, if that makes sense. It’s not satisfying, really. It doesn’t feel like relief afterward. Mostly I just feel confused or frustrated because I realize I’ve paused or repeated something and I don’t understand why.\n\nSo... no, I don’t think I have rituals I feel driven to do. At least not in the way I imagine you mean. It feels less like a ritual and more like I lose the thread of the action for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself making unusual sounds or movements, like twitching or blinking, that you can't control?\n\nPatient: Not really like twitching or blinking, no. I mean, I don’t think I have a tic where my eye keeps going or I make sounds without meaning to. At least nobody’s pointed that out to me.\n\nWhat I do notice is more with my hands at work. Like I’ve said, I’ll be at the register and I’m supposed to be doing a normal thing—scanning, taking money, giving change—and then I get stuck in some movement that doesn’t match the situation. I’ll tap the scanner in this pattern, or I’ll just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting. It’s embarrassing because I know what I’m supposed to be doing, but for those moments I’m not doing it.\n\nSo I don’t know if that counts as an unusual movement. It’s not a sound or a blink, and it doesn’t feel like a twitch exactly. It’s more like my body gets caught doing the wrong thing, or stops halfway through the right thing. That’s the part that got me in trouble at work—last week they wrote me up for it because it looked like I was fooling around or not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms have affected your ability to maintain relationships or perform daily tasks outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes... I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork is the most obvious because there’s paperwork now. I’m on a formal warning, and that feels serious, like it’s not just me worrying that I’m slower or making people impatient. They actually wrote it down, and now I feel like every shift I’m being watched or like I have to prove I can get through basic tasks without messing them up. That’s been hard.\n\nOutside of work, it’s more gradual, but it’s there. I used to keep up with people better. I’d call family, answer messages, make plans with friends. I used to go kayaking when people invited me, or I’d be the one trying to set it up sometimes. Lately I just... don’t. I let texts sit there. I tell myself I’ll call back after dinner, then it’s the next day, then it feels awkward because I waited too long. Same with kayaking plans — I’ll think maybe I should go, it would probably be good for me, but then I don’t follow through.\n\nSo daily tasks outside work aren’t completely impossible, but I’m not functioning the way I used to. I’m more behind on keeping contact with people, and I think some of them have stopped asking as much because I haven’t been reliable. That part bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms have made it harder for you to interact with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I was sort of trying to separate work from the rest of my life in my head, like, “Okay, the register problem is just at work,” but that’s not really true anymore.\n\nI’m on a formal warning at work now, which has made me more tense in general, and then when I’m not working I’ve kind of stopped doing the normal keeping-up-with-people things. I don’t call family the way I used to. I’ll see a message and think I’m going to answer it later, and then I just don’t, or I wait so long that it feels embarrassing to respond. And with friends, especially the kayaking plans, I used to be more involved. I’d go when people asked, or help figure out a day, and now I just let it pass by.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about them. That’s the part I feel bad about. I do care, but I’m not following through, and I think people notice after a while. So yes, interacting with friends and family has definitely gotten harder, mostly because I’m not keeping up with them like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors outside of work to feel a sense of completion or relief?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. Like, outside of work I don’t think I have a thing where I have to touch the doorknob five times or check something over and over until it feels “right,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI do sometimes get stuck trying to finish things, but it’s more like I lose the thread or I sit there longer than I should, not that I’m repeating a behavior for relief. I don’t really get that feeling of, “If I do this action again, then I’ll feel complete.” At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is probably no, or not in a clear way. I may need you to give me an example if there’s a specific kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, everybody has things pop into their head, right? At least… not that I’ve noticed as a pattern.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, or maybe I’m not totally sure what counts. I mean, when you say “compelled to think about,” I don’t really recognize that feeling in myself.\n\nI can get stuck, sure, and sometimes I’ll sit there kind of circling around something in my head longer than I probably should. But it’s not like there’s a specific image or thought that I have to bring up in my mind to make the behavior feel finished, if that makes sense. I’m not thinking, “Okay, I need to picture this,” or “I need to repeat this thought until it feels right.”\n\nMostly it’s more vague than that. Like I might be aware I’m not moving on from something, or I’m taking too long, but I’m not aware of a recurring mental picture or phrase driving it. If there is something happening, I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to describe it. I don’t always like admitting that, because part of me wants to say I’m still managing, I still go to work, I still do what I have to do. But if I’m being honest, things have slipped.\n\nAt work, it’s become serious enough that I’m on a formal warning now. That’s not something I’ve ever wanted attached to my name, and it’s been weighing on me. I try to tell myself, you know, “Just get through the shift, keep your head down, do the job,” but clearly it’s not invisible to other people anymore.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve really pulled back. I used to keep up with friends more. I used to make kayaking plans, or at least be the kind of person who would say yes when someone suggested it. I’d call family more regularly too. Now I keep putting things off, or I don’t respond, or I tell myself I’ll call tomorrow and then it becomes next week. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I just haven’t been keeping up with people or with the things that made my life feel normal.\n\nSo yes, I’d say my quality of life has definitely taken a hit. Not completely collapsed, but… it’s smaller than it used to be, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any unwanted thoughts that keep coming into your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way. I know I sort of answered something like this already, but I’m trying to think if there’s another way to say it.\n\nI do have thoughts come and go, and sometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about things longer than I want to, but I wouldn’t say there are specific unwanted thoughts that keep barging in and that I’m fighting off all day. It’s more like normal worries or random things I remember, and then sometimes I have trouble shifting my attention away from them. But they don’t feel like these repeated intrusive thoughts that I can’t control, if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike, if I’m distracted, I might keep turning something over in my head, but it’s not a particular thought that keeps coming back against my will in the same way every time. I haven’t noticed that pattern. So my answer would be… probably no. Or at least, not something I can identify clearly as that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these episodes of getting stuck or repetitive behaviors are significantly interfering with your ability to function or enjoy daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say they are, at this point. I mean, I can still get through parts of my day, so sometimes I catch myself wanting to downplay it, like, “Well, I’m still showing up, I’m still doing the basics.” But the truth is, it’s interfering enough that other people are noticing.\n\nWork is the clearest example. I’m a cashier, and I’m on a formal warning now. That’s been hard to sit with, because I don’t like feeling like I’m becoming unreliable or like I’m making problems for the people around me. It makes each shift feel heavier, because I’m aware I can’t just keep saying it’s only in my head or only affecting me.\n\nAnd then outside of work, it’s like my life has narrowed. I’ve stopped keeping up with friends the way I used to. Kayaking plans, which used to be something I actually looked forward to, have just kind of fallen away. Family calls too — I’ll think, “I should call,” and then I don’t, and then more time passes, and then it feels awkward, and I put it off again.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely affecting my functioning and my ability to enjoy things. Not every single minute, but enough that my daily life doesn’t feel like it used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it has. Maybe not in the sense that I don’t want people in my life, because I do. I still care about my wife and my kids very much. That hasn’t gone away. But there’s this… lack of push, I guess, or I don’t know what to call it. Like the feeling that should get me to pick up the phone or make a plan or follow through just isn’t there the way it used to be.

My son’s at college, and I’ll think, “I should call him, I should check in, ask how classes are going, see how he’s doing,” and then I just don’t. Not because I don’t care. I really do. I’ll even sit there with the phone nearby sometimes, and it’s like the step between thinking about it and actually doing it feels bigger than it should. Then a few days pass, and then I feel bad, and then it becomes even harder because now I feel like I have to explain why I haven’t called.

Same thing with the kayak group. I used to be able to meet people that way, or at least be around people without it feeling like such a big production. Now even if I know they’re going out, I don’t really get myself there. I don’t make the effort to join in, and after a while people stop asking as much. I can’t really blame them.

And at home, even with small things, like my little repair projects, I’ll have something sitting there that I know I could work on, something I normally might enjoy or at least feel useful doing, and I just don’t start. So it’s not only new relationships. It’s keeping the old connections alive too.

When people ask me what’s wrong, I usually just shrug. I don’t have much to say. It’s not that there’s nothing going on inside, exactly, but I can’t seem to turn it into words, or I don’t have the energy to explain it properly. So yes, I’d say it’s made connecting with people harder. I’m still attached to them, but I’m not reaching out like I should.

It’s not that there’s nothing going on inside, exactly, but I can’t seem to turn it into words, or I don’t have the energy to explain it properly. So yes, I’d say it’s made connecting with people harder. I’m still attached to them, but I’m not reaching out like I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because things have been getting... strange at work, I guess. I’m a cashier, and lately when I’m at the register I keep getting stuck doing things that don’t really fit what’s happening. Like I’ll be scanning items and then I’ll start tapping the scanner in a certain pattern, not because I need to, just... I get caught in it. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just sort of freeze over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting and staring at me.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to just keep moving, ring them up, hand them the receipt, all of that. I’ve done this job long enough. But in the moment I get stuck, like my body is doing the wrong thing or not doing the next thing. I’ll be doing something normal, like scanning items, and then I get caught tapping the scanner in a pattern, like my hand just keeps doing that instead of moving on. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just freeze over the cash drawer, and I know the person is waiting, but I don’t move right away. It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing in that moment.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It got bad enough last week that my manager wrote me up for it, and that made it feel like, okay, this isn’t just me being a little distracted or having a weird day. It’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these episodes of getting stuck happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’d call it anxiety, at least not at the very start of it. It’s more like I suddenly realize I’m not moving forward, and then I become very aware that someone is waiting on me. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. I don’t know if I’m anxious and then I get stuck, or if I get stuck and then the anxiety comes because I can see the situation going wrong. Usually by the time I notice it, I’m already thinking, “Why am I doing this? It’s not like, “I have to tap the scanner three times or something terrible will happen.” At least, I don’t experience it that way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be at the register, in the middle of a normal transaction, and then I get stuck doing something that doesn’t really fit what’s going on. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. Sometimes I’ll keep thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What did that customer think?” but that feels more like worrying about the situation after it happens than some separate repeated thought.\n\nSo... I guess I’d say not really, or I’m not aware of it. I don’t usually have a clear thought like, “I need to repeat this,” or “I have to do it this way.” It’s not like I’m planning a ritual. It just kind of happens, and then once I notice it, I feel stuck in the middle of it and it’s hard to shift back to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nI guess there can be a pull to keep doing whatever my hand is doing for a moment, but I don’t experience it as something meaningful or necessary, if that makes sense. It’s not satisfying, really. It doesn’t feel like relief afterward. Like I’ve said, I’ll be at the register and I’m supposed to be doing a normal thing—scanning, taking money, giving change—and then I get stuck in some movement that doesn’t match the situation. I used to keep up with friends more. I used to make kayaking plans, or at least be the kind of person who would say yes when someone suggested it. I’d call family more regularly too. I just haven’t been keeping up with people or with the things that made my life feel normal.\n\nSo yes, I’d say my quality of life has definitely taken a hit. I know I sort of answered something like this already, but I’m trying to think if there’s another way to say it.\n\nI do have thoughts come and go, and sometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about things longer than I want to, but I wouldn’t say there are specific unwanted thoughts that keep barging in and that I’m fighting off all day. I haven’t noticed that pattern. So my answer would be… probably no. I’m a cashier, and I’m on a formal warning now. That’s been hard to sit with, because I don’t like feeling like I’m becoming unreliable or like I’m making problems for the people around me. It makes each shift feel heavier, because I’m aware I can’t just keep saying it’s only in my head or only affecting me.\n\nAnd then outside of work, it’s like my life has narrowed. I’ve stopped keeping up with friends the way I used to. Kayaking plans, which used to be something I actually looked forward to, have just kind of fallen away. Family calls too — I’ll think, “I should call,” and then I don’t, and then more time passes, and then it feels awkward, and I put it off again.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely affecting my functioning and my ability to enjoy things. Not every single minute, but enough that my daily life doesn’t feel like it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms have made it difficult for you to connect with others or form new relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. Yes… yes, I think it is. I know I’ve sort of circled around this already, but when you ask it that directly, I’d have to say it’s not just bothering me in a minor way. It’s affecting how I’m getting through my day.

It’s not that there’s nothing going on inside, exactly, but I can’t seem to turn it into words, or I don’t have the energy to explain it properly. So yes, I’d say it’s made connecting with people harder. I’m still attached to them, but I’m not reaching out like I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are causing you significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. I know I’ve sort of circled around this already, but when you ask it that directly, I’d have to say it’s not just bothering me in a minor way. It’s affecting how I’m getting through my day.\n\nAt work especially, it’s become real in a way I can’t explain away. I’m a cashier, and I’m on a formal warning now. That’s not something I ever wanted to happen. I keep thinking, you know, I should be able to just go in and do the job, get through the shift, keep things steady. But the fact that it’s reached the point where work has had to officially say something… that feels like a line got crossed. It’s embarrassing, and it makes me anxious going in, because I’m aware that I’m not functioning the way I used to.\n\nAnd then when I’m not at work, I’m not really keeping up with my life either. Friends, kayaking plans, family calls — those things used to be part of the normal rhythm. I wasn’t perfect about it, but I stayed connected. Now I let things slide. I don’t follow through. I’ll think about calling family or seeing people, and then I just don’t do it, and then more time passes.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing impairment. I can still do some things, but my life has gotten smaller, and I’m not keeping up with work or people the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because things have been getting... strange at work, I guess. I’m a cashier, and lately when I’m at the register I keep getting stuck doing things that don’t really fit what’s happening. Like I’ll be scanning items and then I’ll start tapping the scanner in a certain pattern, not because I need to, just... I get caught in it. Or I’ll be making change and my hand will just sort of freeze over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting and staring at me.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to just keep moving, ring them up, hand them the receipt, all of that. I’ve done this job long enough. But in the moment I get stuck, like my body is doing the wrong thing or not doing the next thing. Last week it happened enough that my manager wrote me up, and that’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone instead of just trying to pretend it’s not happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these episodes of getting stuck are affecting your interactions with customers or coworkers outside of just the immediate task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, it has. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. It feels more like panic after I notice I’m stuck, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do certain things in a specific way or repeat certain actions to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I’m doing it because I believe something bad will happen if I don’t, no. It’s not like, “I have to tap the scanner three times or something terrible will happen.” At least, I don’t experience it that way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be at the register, in the middle of a normal transaction, and then I get stuck doing something that doesn’t really fit what’s going on. Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. I don’t usually have a clear thought like, “I need to repeat this,” or “I have to do it this way.” It’s not like I’m planning a ritual. It just kind of happens, and then once I notice it, I feel stuck in the middle of it and it’s hard to shift back to what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nI guess there can be a pull to keep doing whatever my hand is doing for a moment, but I don’t experience it as something meaningful or necessary, if that makes sense. Not every single minute, but enough that my daily life doesn’t feel like it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms have made it difficult for you to connect with others or form new relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. Like the feeling that should get me to pick up the phone or make a plan or follow through just isn’t there the way it used to be.\n\nMy son’s at college, and I’ll think, “I should call him, I should check in, ask how classes are going, see how he’s doing,” and then I just don’t. Not because I don’t care. I really do. I’ll even sit there with the phone nearby sometimes, and it’s like the step between thinking about it and actually doing it feels bigger than it should. Then a few days pass, and then I feel bad, and then it becomes even harder because now I feel like I have to explain why I haven’t called.\n\nSame thing with the kayak group. I used to be able to meet people that way, or at least be around people without it feeling like such a big production. Now even if I know they’re going out, I don’t really get myself there. I don’t make the effort to join in, and after a while people stop asking as much. I can’t really blame them.\n\nAnd at home, even with small things, like my little repair projects, I’ll have something sitting there that I know I could work on, something I normally might enjoy or at least feel useful doing, and I just don’t start. So it’s not only new relationships. It’s keeping the old connections alive too.\n\nWhen people ask me what’s wrong, I usually just shrug. I don’t have much to say. It’s not that there’s nothing going on inside, exactly, but I can’t seem to turn it into words, or I don’t have the energy to explain it properly. So yes, I’d say it’s made connecting with people harder. I’m still attached to them, but I’m not reaching out like I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are causing you significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. I know I’ve sort of circled around this already, but when you ask it that directly, I’d have to say it’s not just bothering me in a minor way. It’s affecting how I’m getting through my day.\n\nAt work especially, it’s become real in a way I can’t explain away. I’m a cashier, and I’m on a formal warning now. That’s not something I ever wanted to happen. I keep thinking, you know, I should be able to just go in and do the job, get through the shift, keep things steady. But the fact that it’s reached the point where work has had to officially say something… that feels like a line got crossed. It’s embarrassing, and it makes me anxious going in, because I’m aware that I’m not functioning the way I used to.\n\nAnd then when I’m not at work, I’m not really keeping up with my life either. Friends, kayaking plans, family calls — those things used to be part of the normal rhythm. I wasn’t perfect about it, but I stayed connected. Now I let things slide. I don’t follow through. I’ll think about calling family or seeing people, and then I just don’t do it, and then more time passes.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing impairment. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s like… an obsessive thought, exactly. At least not in the way I imagine you mean, like I’m thinking, “If I don’t do this, something terrible will happen,” or I’m trying to calm a specific fear. It doesn’t feel that clear to me.

It’s more like I get caught in the action itself. At the register, I’ll be scanning things and then suddenly I’m tapping the scanner in this pattern, or I’ll stop with my hand over the cash drawer, just sort of frozen there, while the customer is waiting. And part of me knows, “This doesn’t fit right now, you need to keep moving,” but it’s like the next step doesn’t happen immediately.

That’s actually why I came in, because it’s started happening in front of people at work. Last week it got me written up, and that really shook me. If it was just me being anxious, I feel like I could maybe explain it to myself better, but it doesn’t always feel connected to a thought. It’s more like I get stuck doing something odd or I pause in the middle of something I’m supposed to be doing.

It doesn’t fit what I’m supposed to be doing in that moment.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. Then, yes, I get tense and embarrassed, because I know it looks strange or slow or like I’m not paying attention.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to tell which comes first. It’s not like, “I have to tap the scanner three times or something terrible will happen.” At least, I don’t experience it that way.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll be at the register, in the middle of a normal transaction, and then I get stuck doing something that doesn’t really fit what’s going on. Like I’ll start tapping the scanner in this little pattern, or my hand will just stop over the cash drawer when I’m supposed to be giving someone their change. And I know the customer is standing there waiting, and I know I should just finish what I’m doing, but for a few moments I’m not moving on the way I’m supposed to.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. It’s been happening enough at work that it’s not just some private habit nobody sees. Last week I got written up for it, because from the outside I guess it looks like I’m messing around or not paying attention. But inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to make things feel “right” or prevent something. Sometimes I’ll keep thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “What did that customer think?” but that feels more like worrying about the situation after it happens than some separate repeated thought.\n\nSo... I’m a cashier, and I’m on a formal warning now. That’s been hard to sit with, because I don’t like feeling like I’m becoming unreliable or like I’m making problems for the people around me. It makes each shift feel heavier, because I’m aware I can’t just keep saying it’s only in my head or only affecting me.\n\nAnd then outside of work, it’s like my life has narrowed. I’m still attached to them, but I’m not reaching out like I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are causing you significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. I know I’ve sort of circled around this already, but when you ask it that directly, I’d have to say it’s not just bothering me in a minor way. It’s affecting how I’m getting through my day.\n\nAt work especially, it’s become real in a way I can’t explain away. I’m a cashier, and I’m on a formal warning now. That’s not something I ever wanted to happen. I keep thinking, you know, I should be able to just go in and do the job, get through the shift, keep things steady. But the fact that it’s reached the point where work has had to officially say something… that feels like a line got crossed. It’s embarrassing, and it makes me anxious going in, because I’m aware that I’m not functioning the way I used to.\n\nAnd then when I’m not at work, I’m not really keeping up with my life either. Friends, kayaking plans, family calls — those things used to be part of the normal rhythm. I wasn’t perfect about it, but I stayed connected. Now I let things slide. I don’t follow through. I’ll think about calling family or seeing people, and then I just don’t do it, and then more time passes.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing impairment. I can still do some things, but my life has gotten smaller, and I’m not keeping up with work or people the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are performing these repetitive behaviors in response to an obsessive thought or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like… an obsessive thought, exactly. At least not in the way I imagine you mean, like I’m thinking, “If I don’t do this, something terrible will happen,” or I’m trying to calm a specific fear. It doesn’t feel that clear to me.\n\nIt’s more like I get caught in the action itself. At the register, I’ll be scanning things and then suddenly I’m tapping the scanner in this pattern, or I’ll stop with my hand over the cash drawer, just sort of frozen there, while the customer is waiting. And part of me knows, “This doesn’t fit right now, you need to keep moving,” but it’s like the next step doesn’t happen immediately.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because it’s started happening in front of people at work. Last week it got me written up, and that really shook me. If it was just me being anxious, I feel like I could maybe explain it to myself better, but it doesn’t always feel connected to a thought. It’s more like I get stuck doing something odd or I pause in the middle of something I’m supposed to be doing."
  }
]
